A vertical movie-poster design in the deep, dusty darkness of an abandoned 1990s Guangzhou cinema. From a vintage 35mm film projector in a high, central projection booth, a powerful beam of light cuts a crisp, vertical column through the gloom. Within this shaft of light, countless motes of dust dance, adding texture and a sense of disrepair. The light falls upon a vast, torn silver screen, its surface marred by rips and peeling edges. Projected onto this damaged canvas are two monumental, high-contrast silhouettes of two mature Chinese women in their late twenties, Jiang and Luo. Their silhouettes are bold and sharply defined, depicting them in an intimate embrace. Luo's silhouette faces the viewer, her profile sharp, head tilted back, her long, dark hair cascading in silhouette waves over her shoulder as one of Jiang's silhouette hands cradles her cheek in a gentle, visible caress. Jiang's silhouette stands behind Luo, her shorter bob hair just visible, her other arm wrapped around Luo's waist in a secure, possessive hold. Their bodies press together, curves blending into a single, unified shadow shape against the bright projection light, creating a graphic tension between decay and desire. In the projection booth itself, the third woman, Zhen, stands in partial shadow. She is a Chinese woman in her late twenties with a sinewy, athletic dancer's build, her head shaved close. She wears simple, slightly worn work clothes: a thin, sleeveless undershirt that clings to her torso, revealing her form, and dark, loose-fitting trousers. Her expression is one of focused intensity, her sharp eyes fixed on the screen as her hand rests decisively on a large control lever of the projector, actively shaping the image. The projector's light bathes her face and shoulders, highlighting the defined lines of her jaw and collarbone. The composition is one of radical verticality, dominated by the central light beam and the towering silhouettes. The palette is desaturated, dominated by deep charcoal blacks, sepia-tinged dust tones, and the stark, cool white of the projection light. The typography is integrated into this architectural space. The title, 'THE PROJECTED FLESH', is set in a bold, condensed sans-serif type and appears vertically along the right edge of the poster, running parallel to the light beam. The letters are partially transparent, allowing the cinema's decaying wall texture to show through, as if they are projected or stenciled onto the brick. The tagline, 'THEIR TRUEST FILM HAS ONLY ONE SCREENING', is set in a smaller, elegant serif and curves along the bottom left edge of the torn screen. The release line 'COMING SOON' is tucked into the lower right corner, and a compact, fictional English billing block, set in a tiny, clean typeface, is vertically aligned along the left edge, mimicking architectural details. The entire design feels like a single, captured moment of creation and control within the forgotten space.;

Zhen's hand, slightly larger and more muscular with a dancer's strength, rests on top, her fingers interlacing with Jiang's in a deliberate, tender grip, their skin and knuckles rendered in sharp, tactile detail. The letters are massive, occupying the upper two-thirds of the poster. Crucially, the women's clasped hands and the strip of film physically interrupt the letterforms. The edge of the filmstrip cuts cleanly through the horizontal bar of the 'F', their intertwined fingers partially obscure the counter of the 'O', and the shadowed space between their wrists nestles into the bottom curve of the 'U', creating a precise, layered depth where the human element is inseparable from the typography. The background of the poster is the texture of the rough paper and the subtle, faded ghost of the cinema's torn screen, suggested by soft, grainy halftone dots. The central, dominant module is a black-and-white photograph showing the three Chinese women in their early thirties: Inspector Ma, with her severe bun, wears a partially unbuttoned white shirt; Luo, with her sleek undercut, is in a dark tank top; Dr. Chen, with long dark hair, is in a simple camisole. They are in an intimate, re-created pose from a Polaroid, their bodies in close contact-Luo's hand rests on the small of Dr. Chen's back, while Inspector Ma's arm is draped over Luo's shoulder, their cheeks nearly touching, expressions focused, serious, yet with a soft intensity. Surrounding this central image are nine smaller pinned Polaroid prints, each a crisp color close-up of non-human evidence: a rumpled silk sheet, a wristwatch on a nightstand, a pair of eyeglasses, lipstick on a wine glass, a door key, a tangled necklace, a manuscript page, a city map with a circled location, and a single silk stocking. Each Polaroid has a small white evidence tag with typed numbers. Red string connects some Polaroid corners to handwritten annotations on cream-colored forensic report fragments, creating a precise, asymmetric web. The palette is muted: dark green board, black-and-white central photo, the warm cream of the reports, and the stark red of the strings. Lighting is flat and even, as under evidence-room fluorescents, casting soft, distinct shadows from the pinned elements. The English typography is integrated into the grid. The release line 'COMING SOON' is stamped in red ink on a small, rectangular evidence label pinned near the bottom right corner. A compact, fictional billing block in a tiny, legible sans-serif font is printed on a long, narrow strip of photographic paper pinned along the very bottom edge. The overall texture suggests a real corkboard with subtle grain and pin-hole imperfections. Their concealed forms are entangled in an intimate, supportive embrace, a silent tableau of desire and conspiracy. The lighting is stark and directional, from the top left, making the wet paint gleam and casting sharp, defining shadows. This poster radicalizes the embroidery concept through a typographic monolith and macro-to-epic scale. The composition is a severe vertical study in texture and restraint. The entire lower two-thirds of the frame is a monumental, abstracted close-up of three intertwined female forms, rendered not with photographic skin but as a dense, tactile topography of woven silk threads. The threads follow the contours of shoulders, the dip of a waist, the swell of a hip, and the meeting of thighs, creating a continuous, anatomically coherent landscape of flesh made from craft. Specific details anchor the fiction: the disciplined pattern of Shu's coiled hair is suggested by tightly wound black threads; Ying's signature long braid is a thick, intricate plait of threads trailing off the edge; Li's robust shoulders are built from densely packed, sturdy stitches. At the center of this woven tapestry, a single, vivid point of contact is magnified: Ying's slender hand, its fingers splayed, is pressed palm-down against the small of Li's back, while Li's own strong hand overlaps, its thumb stroking Ying's wrist. The palette is a sophisticated duotone of deep indigo threads on a cream silk ground, with the single touch-point highlighted by a shock of crimson thread. From this woven landscape, the poster's dominant typographic monolith erupts. The title 'THE KNOTTED LINE' is constructed from the same thick, physical silk threads, emerging from the textile surface and rising into the stark white upper third of the poster. The letters are not flat but appear embossed, casting soft shadows. The 'O's in 'KNOTTED' are formed by empty embroidery hoops, through which a glimpse of the tangled thread landscape below is visible. The tagline is set in a crisp, modern sans-serif, running vertically along the left edge, interacting with the threads as if being stitched through. The 'COMING SOON' line and the fictional billing block are integrated as tiny, disciplined labels at the very bottom, printed in a deep indigo that matches the threads. The light is flat and even, emphasizing texture over volume, making every twist and knot of thread legible.
